L1091: Source: Applicant submission.
L1092: Reviewer comment: This small, proof-of-concept, open-label study does not contribute to the safety database for this drug development program. Additionally, the AE data are compromised due to the intrasubject control study design; the control gel half of the wound could be contaminated by the study drug wound half, so that relatedness cannot be assessed.
L1093: ## 8.2.8.2 Study AHV-18-A
L1094: Study AHV-18-A was conducted to assess the wound healing effects and tolerability of the control gel which was used during the clinical studies. On the nondominant forearm of twelve healthy volunteers, three 8 mm abrasive wound sites were created, each site was treated with control gel, petrolatum, or no treatment for 21 days. For the primary endpoint, the mean days to complete wound healing were similar for all three treatment groups: 13.3 days for untreated control wounds and wounds treated with control gel; 13.4 days for wounds treated with petrolatum.
L1095: The majority of subjects (75%) reported sixteen TEAEs, fourteen of which were papules. Seven papule events in the wound area were considered to have be possibly or probably related to study procedures. All were mild in severity and resolved by the end of the study.
L1096: *Reviewer comment: The control gel served as a comparator for Filsuvez during trial BEB-13, but is not a vehicle. Carnauba wax, a component of the control gel which is not in the study drug gel, is a botanical and GRAS. It is unclear how this study with intrasubject control design in healthy volunteers and induced wounds provides information regarding control gel for use in the phase 3 pivotal study.*
L1097: ## 8.2.8.3 Study AHV-18-B
L1098: When the Applicant submitted NDA on March 30, 2021, Study AHV-18-B had not been initiated. The Agency sent an IR to the Applicant requesting status and information regarding Study AHV-18-B. On December 30, 2021, the Applicant submitted the study report and results from the study.
L1099: Study AHV-18-B (EudraCT Number: 2020-002358-26) was a randomized, intra-individual controlled trial of the cutaneous healing properties of petrolatum versus the vehicle for Oleogel-S10 when applied topically to mechanically induced partial thickness wounds in healthy volunteers
L1100: The objective of this phase 2 study was to demonstrate that the control gel was non-inferior to petrolatum, based on the primary endpoint of days until complete healing assessed by clinical score.
L1101: The study population was sixteen subjects (8 male and 8 female) aged 18 to 45 years, allowing an additional three subjects for possible subject withdrawals.
L1102: Control Gel ingredients per 100 g: sunflower oil 85 g, bees wax yellow 5 g, carnauba wax 10 g compared to Petrolatum (ALLERGIKA® – BASISSALBE) ingredients per 100 g: white petrolatum 95 g, liquid paraffin 5 g.
L1103: The investigational area was identified on the non-dominant forearm for each subject. The procedure was as follows:
L1104: - Two mini-zones (A and B), were defined by a standardized flexible template, 2 circular holes of 8 mm diameter, 5 cm apart, and labeled using skin marker
- Zones A and B abrasive wounds were created using a sterile scrub brush (such as MEDI-SCRUB, Rovers Medical Devices B.V., Netherlands) after training
- The skin areas were uniformly scrubbed until first signs of epidermal removal, punctuate bleeding visible
- Each site was randomly assigned to control gel or petrolatum application (1 treatment per wound area [A and B]) once daily for 16 days.
- A non-adhesive wound dressing with a non-adherent pad was applied to each wound once a day until Day 10.
- The study was outcome-assessor blinded.
- Study personnel who administered the intervention and the subjects who received the interventions were not blinded.
L1105: ## Results:
L1106: Sixteen patients were randomized, and all completed the study. Eight subjects were randomized to receive control gel on Wound A and petrolatum on Wound B, and 8 subjects were randomized to receive petrolatum on Wound A and control gel on Wound B.
L1107: The mean (standard deviation [SD]) days to complete wound healing assessed by clinical score was 14.2 (3.85) days for wounds treated with control gel and 12.5 (1.75) days for wounds treated with petrolatum. The null hypothesis that the control gel is inferior to petrolatum (mean difference \ge the predefined NIM of 1 day) could not be rejected (upper limit of the 97.5% CI for the difference between treatments was 3.44 days).
L1108: - 100% of the wounds treated with petrolatum healed completely by Day 16
- 87.5% control gel wounds healed completely by Day 16 (14/16 wounds)
- Per protocol, at the next assessment of open wounds at End of Study Visit (Day 23 \pm 2 days), both unhealed wounds treated with control gel had healed at the End of Study Visit.
L1109: ## Safety:
L1110: Ten treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) were reported by six (37.5%) control gel-treated subjects during the study. No wounds treated with petrolatum reported a TEAE.
L1111: Six TEAEs were associated with control gel treatment, reported by 3 (18.8%) subjects:
L1112: - 1 application site pain
- 1 application site pruritus
- 4 application site erosion events
L1113: - scratching of wound site (2 events)
- excoriation due to scratching (2 events)
L1114: Nine TEAEs were considered mild; one report of application site pruritus was considered moderate in severity. None was considered serious. No TEAE led to discontinuation of study treatment. With the exception of one application site pruritus event, all were considered to have a reasonable causal relationship to control gel.
L1115: *Reviewer comments: The null hypothesis could not be rejected; the study results demonstrated that the petrolatum wounds healed faster than the control gel wounds. All the TEAEs and local skin reactions occurred in the control gel wounds. Due to the overall protocol design, including intra-subject control and the safety results in healthy subjects, it is not evident how this study provides support for the birch triterpenes application for EB wound healing.*
L1116: ## 8.2.9. Additional Safety Explorations
L1117: ## Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
L1118: Human carcinogenicity or tumor development is discussed under Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues, Skin Cancer 8.2.5.2.
L1119: ## Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
L1120: During the BEB-13 double blind period, one pregnancy was reported in the control drug group, only. No pregnancy was exposed to study drug. No studies have been conducted in humans assessing pregnancy outcome.
L1121: ## Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1122: On August 7, 2014, the FDA Orphan Drug Designation program granted dry extract from Betulae Cortex (birch bark) orphan status (OD#14-4381). Orphan drugs and biologics are defined as those intended for the treatment, prevention or diagnosis of a rare disease or condition, which is one that affects less than 200,000 persons in the US. PREA does not apply to orphan drug development programs; therefore, no trial specific for the pediatric population was conducted. Filsuvez is intended for use in the pediatric population, and the majority of the enrolled subjects was &lt; 18 years.
L1123: ## Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
L1124: Birch triterpenes does not present the potential for drug abuse, withdrawal and rebound with use. Overdose with Filsuvez may have the potential for administration site effects such as local irritation or pain.
L1125: ## 8.2.10. Safety in the Postmarketing Setting
L1126: ## Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarketing Experience
L1127: Birch triterpenes is a new molecular entity yet to be approved in the U.S., so there is no US postmarketing safety experience.
L1128: Approved by the EMA in January 2016, birch triterpenes gel (trade name Episalvan) has been exclusively manufactured and marketed in the UK since November 2018. On April 8, 2021, the Applicant provided a postmarketing update through the Annual Report submitted to IND 121320. Any newly occurring adverse events (AEs) and the cumulative summary of AEs provided limited new information, and did not identify a new safety signal or lead to labeling changes.
L1129: ## 8.2.11. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L1130: The safety of birch triterpenes was assessed through a single pivotal study BEB-13, consisting of a 90-day double-blind period and a 24-month open-label study period. For the EB indication, there was no additional phase 3 trial data to pool or combine for analysis.
L1131: ## 8.3. Statistical Issues
L1132: Study BEB-13 was a randomized, double-blind, parallel group, vehicle-controlled study in subjects with EB. The study included an unblinded sample size re-estimation after 50% enrollment. The sample size was increased following the interim analysis, but not to the full amount possible under the protocol. Because of the planned sample size re-assessment, the primary analysis was adjusted using the method of Cui, Hung, and Wang. The primary endpoint met the statistical significance criteria. Secondary endpoints were to be analyzed in sequential order, but because the first endpoint did not demonstrate statistical significance, none of the remaining secondary endpoints could be formally tested. However, none of the secondary endpoints were even nominally statistically significant.
L1133: The supportive studies (BEB-10, BBW-11, BSG-12, and BSH-12) all have significant design flaws that could introduce bias. Thus, the trends favoring Oleogel-S10 over the control treatment in these studies are not convincing enough to provide confirmatory evidence of efficacy to support the primary study (BEB-13).
L1134: ## 8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
L1135: The applicant conducted one randomized, double-blind, parallel-group, vehicle-controlled trial in subjects with DEB and JEB. Study BEB-13 demonstrated a statistically significant effect at the 0.05 level for the primary efficacy endpoint of the proportion of subjects with first complete closure of the target wound within 45 days (p=0.013). However, none of the prespecified secondary efficacy endpoints demonstrated statistical significance. The secondary endpoints related to the primary endpoint (time to first closure of the target wound and proportion of subjects with first complete closure of the target wound within 90 days) had smaller effects in favor of Oleogel-S10 over control, but they were not statistically significant. The primary endpoint results were consistent across gender and race subgroups, but subgroups such as adult subjects, subjects with JEB and DDEB, and subjects enrolled in the U.S. did not have trends favoring Oleogel-S10 over control, though all of these subgroups were too small to reliably estimate a treatment effect. For the key secondary endpoints based on assessment of itch or blistering/ erosions/ crusting and the other secondary endpoints based on pain or treatment satisfaction, none of the endpoints had consistent trends favoring Oleogel-S10. Thus, the efficacy conclusions for Study BEB-13 rely exclusively on the effect observed for the primary efficacy endpoint, without support from the secondary endpoints. The findings of Study BEB-13 are not sufficiently robust with persuasive statistical and clinically meaningful findings to support a single-study submission. In addition, the study was not powered for a significance level smaller than 0.05 as is recommended when efficacy will be evaluated in a single study.
L1136: The applicant conducted a small proof-of-concept study in subjects with EB (Study BEB-10). Although the efficacy results appear to lean towards a greater benefit to Oleogel-S10 relative to wound dressing alone, Study BEB-10 has significant design flaws that make it difficult to rule out whether the results could be driven by bias. The study was a small single-center proof-of-concept study conducted prior to Study BEB-13. The most fundamental design flaw was the lack of randomization for the treatment assignment for the wound halves or pairs. When the investigator has discretion as to which wound half to assign the active treatment, bias is a significant concern that could overwhelm any observed treatment effect. In addition, open-label treatment assignments can lead to differences in behavior or compliance regarding the separate treatment zones possibly impacting the results. Finally, the efficacy endpoint was subjective without clearly defined success criteria (faster re-epithelialization). Using photographs can make it challenging to adequately evaluate the wound healing process due to variations in lighting, subject positioning, and the inability to view the wound at multiple angles. Thus, the design flaws, particularly the lack of randomization, are of such significance that the study is not adequate for providing confirmatory evidence for the efficacy of Oleogel-S10.
L1137: The applicant has also conducted one study in subjects with partial thickness burn wounds (Study BBW-11) and two studies in subjects with split-thickness skin graft donor sites (Studies BSG-12 and BSH-12). All three studies had an open-label, intra-individual design with blinded photographic assessors. Unlike Study BEB-10, these studies did incorporate randomized treatment assignment for the split or matched wounds. However, they have comparable design
L1138: issues as Study BEB-10 due to the open-label, split-wound design with photographic efficacy assessment. All three studies had substantial proportions of subjects who had photos that were not suitable for blinded assessment or had censored times because the blinded evaluators could not identify epithelialization on at least one wound half. Thus, the results of analyses based on means and confidence intervals from heavily censored and imputed data are not reliable. Because partial thickness burn wounds and split-thickness skin graft donor site wounds have substantial differences from EB wounds, and the study designs of these studies have significant limitations, these studies are not adequate for providing confirmatory evidence for the efficacy of Oleogel-S10 in the treatment of EB.
L1139: Thus, while the applicant has conducted one study in EB subjects with a randomized, double-blind, controlled, parallel-group design and favorable efficacy results on the primary efficacy endpoint, the secondary endpoints in the study do not provide additional support for efficacy observed on the primary endpoint. All of the supportive studies have split-wound, open-label designs with blinded photographic assessment for efficacy that could be subject to significant bias that make these studies not suitable for providing confirmatory evidence. During the development of Oleogel-S10, FDA advised the applicant that conducting only one successful pivotal trial may not be adequate for establishing efficacy, and that if they intended to establish efficacy on a single Phase 3 trial, that the findings would need to be robust with persuasive statistical and clinically meaningful findings. The applicant has submitted one study in subjects with EB that met the statistical significance criteria for the primary endpoint at \alpha=0.05, but none of the secondary endpoints provided supportive evidence of efficacy. Because the study results from Study BEB-13 were not overwhelmingly convincing and none of the supportive studies were adequate, additional confirmatory evidence is needed to provide substantial evidence of efficacy for Oleogel-S10 in the treatment of EB.

L1142: ## Other External Meetings:

The PC Project and debra led the meeting as part of the FDA Externally-Led Patient-Focused Drug Development (EL-PFDD) initiative. The PC Project prepared the transcript for the PC presentations, and the EB portion was not transcribed in detail. The video from the EL-PFDD live stream was available online and reviewed.

L1170: ## 14 Division Director (DPT-II) Comments

L1172: ## 15 Division Director (OCP) Comments
L1173: ## 16 Division Director (OB) Comments
L1174: ## 17 Division Director (Clinical) Comments
L1175: I concur with the review team's recommendation to issue a Complete Response Letter, because the available efficacy data are not sufficient to meet substantial evidence of effectiveness, on the basis of a single, adequate and well-controlled trial with inadequate confirmatory evidence.
L1176: Wound healing is a complex, dynamic, "highly ordered sequence of events, finely tuned by the molecular crosstalk between different cell populations at the wound site, that guarantees efficient recovery of skin homeostasis" (Cianfarani 2017). Several authors have described sequential, overlapping phases of wound healing, such as hemostasis, inflammation, cell proliferation and tissue remodeling. I concur with the general premise that the biological process of wound healing should be the same, especially with respect to otherwise healthy populations. However, it may not necessarily follow that therapy for wound healing in healthy populations can be extrapolated to conditions of impaired wound healing as in epidermolysis bullosa (EB).
L1177: Epidermolysis bullosa (EB) comprises a rare, heterogeneous group of genetic skin fragility disorders, characterized by blistering and erosions of epithelial surfaces in response to minor trauma/friction. There are four EB types, based on the ultrastructural location of skin cleavage: EB simplex (basal layer of epidermis), dystrophic EB (dermis), junctional EB (dermal-epidermal junction) and Kindler Syndrome (multiple cleavage planes). Dystrophic EB (DEB) is the second most common EB subtype and the most disabling (Conderelli 2019).
L1178: Recessive DEB (RDEB) patients comprised a majority of EB subjects enrolled in the single Phase 3 trial BEB-13. RDEB depends on defective production of collagen 7 (C7), that determines blister formation and impaired wound healing (Cianfarani 2017) and distinguishes DEB from other partial-thickness wounds with normal underlying collagen production, e.g., burn wounds
L1179: ## Filsuvez® (birch triterpenes) Gel
L1180: and partial-thickness surgical wounds. In addition, bacterial colonization of frequently denuded skin may lead to biofilm formation and impairment of cell functions (Cianfarani). Loss of the structural function of C7 also leads to scarring in other organs, including the GI tract, resulting in nutritional deficiency and anemia. Nutritional deficiency may also impair wound healing in DEB. For these reasons, burn wounds and surgical partial-thickness wounds do not seem to be ideal models for EB as these other wounds do not approximate the wound healing impairment in EB.
L1181: Filsuvez gel is a botanical drug product consisting of the drug substance birch triterpenes (dry extract) from birch bark, quantified to 72-88% betulin, [REDACTED](b) (4) and refined sunflower oil. The drug product is a sterile, colorless to slightly yellowish, opalescent, nonaqueous gel. Each gram of Filsuvez gel 10% contains 100 mg birch triterpenes in a base of sunflower oil. Additional specified components of the botanical product are: [REDACTED](b) (4) % (w/w) Lupol, [REDACTED](b) (4) % (w/w) Betulinic acid, [REDACTED](b) (4) % Erythrodiol, and [REDACTED](b) (4) % (w/w) Oleanolic acid. Betulin can be found in some plant-based foods (e.g., olives, lingonberries).
L1182: The applicant has proposed an indication of accelerated wound healing in dystrophic and junctional EB. There is no claim that Filsuvez specifically affects C7.
L1183: The main evidence of effectiveness is based on a single, randomized, double-blind, controlled2 trial, BEB-13. Study BEB-13 demonstrated a statistically significant effect at the 0.05 level for the primary efficacy endpoint, the proportion of subjects with first complete closure of the target wound within 45 days (p=0.013). However, analyses of missing data handling indicate that the statistical significance depends on assumptions made (e.g., worst case vs. multiple imputation) and the tipping point analyses suggest that the statistical significance is marginal.
L1184: Key secondary endpoints, including median days to first complete closure (92 days for Filsuvez and 94 days for control) and first complete closure within 90 days (N=55 [50.5%] for Filsuvez and N=50 [43.9%] for control) were favorable for Filsuvez with small differences that were not statistically significant.
L1185: In evaluating whether the observed favorable effects for Filsuvez in BEB-13 were clinically meaningful, assessment of symptoms (itch and background and procedural pain scores) associated with EB wounds did not support a clinically meaningful treatment effect for Filsuvez. The incidence of target wound infection was low in both groups (2% or n/N=2/109 in Filsuvez-treated subjects vs. 4% or n/N=5/114 in control-treated subjects) and, while in a favorable direction for Filsuvez, are difficult to interpret because of the low numbers of events.
L1186: 2 BEB-13 did not use a true vehicle control. AHV-18A and AHV-18B were designed to evaluate the effect of the control gel on wound healing in healthy adult subjects.
L1187: Thus, while BEB-13 met its primary endpoint, the results are not sufficiently robust with persuasive statistical and clinically meaningful findings to support a single-study submission.
L1188: The applicant submitted four studies to provide confirmatory evidence of effectiveness; only one of these studies (BEB-10) was conducted in EB subjects. These four studies utilized an open-label and split-wound design with photographs interpreted by blinded readers.
L1189: The review team has highlighted significant design flaws with the small single-site proof of concept study in EB subjects (BEB-10), including open-label, nonrandomized treatment assignment; lack of standardization of photographs; and subjective primary efficacy endpoint without defined success criteria. I concur that BEB-10 cannot be relied upon to provide confirmatory evidence.
L1190: The applicant also submitted three studies conducted in subjects with burn wounds and split-thickness donor wounds as confirmatory evidence from other populations; as noted above, it is not clear that the results of these studies can be extrapolated to the EB population. Moreover, all three studies had substantial proportions of subjects who had photos that were not suitable for blinded assessment or had censored times because the blinded evaluators could not identify epithelialization on at least one wound half. The results of analyses based on means and confidence intervals from heavily censored and imputed data are not reliable. Because of these concerns, the three burn and split-thickness donor wound studies are not adequate to serve as confirmatory evidence of effectiveness and additional confirmatory evidence is needed to support effectiveness.
L1191: ## Safety:
L1192: The safety review is largely based on the controlled data from BEB-13. Additional subjects were exposed to birch triterpenes in BEB-10 and the burn wound/split-thickness donor wound trials; however, because of the split-wound designs of these trials, each subject received both control and IP, making it difficult to evaluate safety of the investigational product (vs. background rates) with respect to systemic AEs.
L1193: The primary safety population in BEB-13 consisted of a total of 223 EB subjects, of which 109 were treated with study drug. Filsuvez appears to be well-tolerated, as about 92% of subjects on investigational product completed the double-blind period. In the controlled population, treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were most frequently reported in the Injury, Poisoning, and Procedural Complications system organ class (SOC), and wound complication was the most commonly reported TEAE in this SOC. Wound complication, including wound reopening and wound enlargement, was reported in 80 (73.4 %) Filsuvez subjects and 80 (70.2%) control gel subjects (i.e., numerically similar between groups). Filsuvez does not have significant potential for irritancy, contact sensitization or photosensitivity reactions.
L1194: ## Filsuvez® (birch triterpenes) Gel
L1195: During the double-blind period, serious adverse events were reported in seven (6.4%) subjects treated with Filsuvez and six (5.3%) subjects treated with control gel (numerically similar between groups). The SAE anemia was reported for 3 (2.8%) subjects in the Filsuvez group and not reported (0%) in the control group. Anemia was reported in six Filsuvez-treated subjects and three control-treated subjects with RDEB. Anemia is a known complication of RDEB and it is not clear whether there is a relationship with treatment, given the small numerical differences between groups. A review of means and shift tables for red blood cell counts and hemoglobin and hematocrit levels (Day 0 to Day 90) collected in BEB-13 showed small changes without clear relevance.
L1196: ## 18 Office Director Comments
L1197: I concur with the recommendation of the Division of Dermatology and Dentistry to issue a Complete Response for NDA 215064 for Filsuvez (birch triterpenes) gel. Filsuvez, a new molecular entity, is a topical gel containing five pentacyclic triterpenes isolated from birch tree bark; the product is thought to modulate inflammatory responses implicated in wound healing. Filsuvez is proposed to accelerate wound healing in adult and pediatric patients with epidermolysis bullosa (EB), a group of rare, genetic, heterogeneous skin fragility disorders for which there are currently no approved therapies.
L1198: Efficacy was assessed in a single, randomized, vehicle-controlled trial in patients with junctional or dystrophic EB. While the primary endpoint for Study BEB-13 was met, that is, Filsuvez treatment resulted in an increased proportion of subjects with first complete closure of the EB target wound within 45 days relative to control gel, there are insufficient data in the application to support the conclusion that Filsuvez treatment reliably results in accelerated wound healing or reduced total wound burden. Concerns regarding the results from this trial include, 1) the pre-specified and exploratory secondary endpoints provide limited support for a substantial benefit with active treatment, 2) the trajectory of treatment effects for Filsuvez and control gel appear similar from Day 0 to Day 90 for total body wound burden, body surface area percentage, itch, procedural pain and background pain, suggesting that there were no meaningful differences between treatment arms, 3) at day 45, when the primary endpoint was measured, mean procedural pain scores were lower for control gel than for Filsuvez, and 4) patient satisfaction at day 90 was reported as greater on control gel than on Filsuvez. Results from a single center, proof-of-concept, open label study conducted in ten EB patients are difficult to interpret given the lack of randomization and split-wound design.
L1199: ## Filsuvez® (birch triterpenes) Gel
L1200: There are no data to support a mechanism of action for Filsuvez in EB. The short-term in vitro studies referenced in this application used cells cultured from healthy volunteers; there are no data on the drug's effect on the basement membrane defects in EB. While the product has modest effects on non-EB partial thickness wounds, I agree with the Division that it is not possible to extrapolate the results of trials conducted in subjects with burn wounds and split-thickness donor wounds to patients with EB.
L1201: Filsuvez gel treatment was well tolerated during the double-blinded portion of Study BEB-13 and during the open label extension phase. The incidence of target wound infection was low.
L1202: The efficacy findings of Study BEB-13 and the other submitted trials are not sufficiently robust to support a determination of substantial evidence of effectiveness for Filsuvez in EB based on a single adequate and well controlled study with confirmatory evidence. Without substantial evidence of effectiveness of Filsuvez in this patient population, a determination about the overall clinical benefit of the drug cannot be made. An additional clinical trial to demonstrate the efficacy of Filsuvez in EB will be needed.

L1221: ## 19.4. OCP Appendices (Technical documents supporting OCP recommendations)
L1222: The EB clinical development program of Oleogel-S10 is supported by one phase 2 and one pivotal phase 3 trial in subjects \ge 21 days of age with inherited EB as listed below:
L1223: - Study BEB-10: a phase 2 trial in subjects 1 to 95 years of age with inherited EB (n=10)
- Study BEB-13: a phase 3 trial in subjects \ge 21 days of age with inherited EB (n=223)
L1224: Dedicated clinical pharmacology studies were not conducted for this NDA as Filsuvez has been granted orphan drug designation for the treatment of EB, a group of rare diseases that cause the skin to become very fragile. Systemic exposure to betulin, however, was assessed in the pivotal phase 3 trial (BEB-13) with sparse PK sampling.
L1225: The Applicant has also characterized the absorption, distribution, and elimination properties of betulin in vitro by using human biomaterials.
L1226: ## 19.4.1. Determination of Betulin Concentrations in Human Blood
L1227: The liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) assay for determination of blood concentrations of betulin (the main component of the active substance birch triterpenes) in subjects was developed and validated.
L1228: For EB patients, of whom a high proportion are pediatric patients, a minimally invasive blood sample collection method was preferred. Therefore, initially, in the pivotal phase 3 trial BEB-13, betulin was analyzed in dried blood spots (DBS) obtained from capillary whole blood samples. However, there was poor uptake of optional finger/heel prick and potential contamination, so the protocol was later amended to also obtain whole blood samples from venous blood, primarily taken from safety lab samples. These venous blood samples were treated identically to DBS samples for bioanalytical purposes. Briefly, cards containing the blood samples were dried for a minimum of 2 hours and 6 mm spots were punched out for the assay. The samples were reconstituted by soaking in an organic solution with the internal standard in an ultrasonic bath. The samples were then extracted with Hexane/Dichloromethane, dried, and reconstituted prior to quantitation by LC-MS/MS. The validated lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) of this assay was 10.0 ng/mL and the calibrated linear range is 10.0 to 250 ng/mL. Accuracy and
L1229: precision were monitored during the validation, standard curve performance, and with prepared quality control samples were generally within acceptable limits. The long-term frozen stability of betulin is up to 1 year and this was deemed adequate.
L1230: Of note, betulin was reported to be presented in olives and some fruits. In the Oleogel-S10 treatment group of Study BEB-13, one subject (b)(6) had a baseline betulin concentration (from capillary blood sampling) of 122 ng/mL, which is higher than the highest background value of 43.9 ng/mL seen in the earlier Oleogel-S10 studies in subjects with split-thickness skin graft donor sites and burn wounds (BSH-12, BSG-12, and BBW-11). It is noted that there were no dietary restrictions in the Phase 3 trial.
L1231: ## 19.4.1.1. Betulin Systemic Exposure in Capillary and Venous Blood Samples
L1232: Betulin systemic exposures obtained from capillary and venous blood samples of Study BEB-13 are summarized in Table 48 and Table 49, respectively. In the capillary blood samples, the mean or median values of betulin concentrations in the Oleogel-S10 treatment group were generally higher than those in the control gel (placebo) group (Table 3). However, in the venous blood samples, the concentrations of betulin in the Oleogel-S10 treatment group were comparable to those in the control gel (placebo) group (Table 49). The reason for the higher concentrations in the capillary sampling is unknown, although it is likely due to sample contamination from Oleogel-S10 at the site of the finger stick process may have contributed to these high concentrations.
L1233: Notably, several capillary samples in the Oleogel-S10 treatment group resulted in unexplained high concentrations of betulin. Six of 26 subjects (27%) who provided capillary blood samples had betulin concentrations of &gt;1000 ng/mL at one and/or more timepoints assessed (Table 50). Whereas the venous betulin concentrations in the 3 of these 6 subjects were below 36 ng/mL; in the remaining 3 subjects, venous concentrations were either not quantifiable or not measured (Table 50). No cross-analysis comparisons of betulin concentrations between capillary and venous sampling was conducted yet due to very limited data and the fact that doses and sampling times were not consistent.

L1250: Overall, based on limited capillary blood samples and the potential for contamination with the capillary blood sampling by finger or heel stick, the concentrations of betulin obtained from venous blood samples are considered more reliable data to estimate the systemic exposure of betulin in Study BEB-13.
L1251: Following topical treatment of Filsuvez gel once daily, every 2 or 3 days or once weekly with a mean treatment area of BSA 12% or affected wound surface area of 1100 cm2 at baseline, betulin venous blood concentrations in approximately 70% evaluable subjects were BLQ of 10 ng/mL during the double-blind phase of Study BEB-13, suggesting that following topical treatment of Filsuvez gel, systemic absorption of betulin is limited or poor. Furthermore, systemic exposure of betulin was neither correlated with age (Figure 11), body-mass index (MBI) (Figure 11), nor with total wound area (Filsuvez dose) (Figure 13). The highest concentrations of betulin in adults and pediatrics were 33 ng/mL and 207 ng/mL, respectively.

L1262: ## 19.4.2.2. Metabolism
L1263: - In vitro phenotyping studies (G-A-VIT-19-059 and G-A-VIT-19-060): CYP3A contributes the large majority of the CYP-mediated pathway, while contributions of CYP2C8 and CYP2C19 to the metabolism of betulin are negligible (Table 51 and Table 52). However, non-CYP enzymes were found to play the predominant role in the hepatic metabolism of betulin, most likely phase II conjugation enzymes.
- Metabolic profile in hepatocytes (G-A-VIT-19-050): The in vitro study indicated that incubation of betulin 1 \mumol/L with cryopreserved hepatocytes from humans generated at least 4 metabolites (Figure 14).

L1274: Figure 14: Proposed Metabolic Pathways of Betulin
L1275: Figure 14 illustrates the proposed metabolic pathways of Betulin, showing the conversion of Betulin into several metabolites (M2, M3, M4, M5) in different species (mouse, minipig).

L1277: ## 19.4.2.3. Drug-Drug Interactions
L1278: - CYP induction study (G-A-VIT-19-058): Betulin up to 10\mu\text{M} (4427 ng/mL) did not induce CYP 1A2, 2B6, or 3A4.
- CYP inhibition study (G-A-VIT-19-057): In vitro, betulin inhibited CYP 2C8 and 3A with an \text{IC}_{50} value of \ge 0.17 \mu\text{M} (75 ng/mL).
L1279: ## 19.5. Additional Efficacy Assessment Scales
L1280: ## Leuven Itch Scale
L1281: The Leuven Itch Scale was assessed in subjects age 14 or older in Study BEB-13. The scale assesses 6 domains (frequency, duration, severity, consequences, distress, and surface area). Each domain is scored and analyzed separately. Subjects either recorded responses using an ordinal scale or a visual analog scale (VAS). Scores for duration, severity, consequences, distress, and surface area were only recorded if the frequency subscore is greater than 0. Responses on each domain are scaled to 0 to 100. The six domains are scored as follows.
L1282: (Source: BEB-13 Protocol pg. 57-59, BEB-13 Statistical Analysis Plan pg. 39-41, and annotated CRF pg. 30-32)
L1283: ## *Itch frequency*
L1284: How often subjects experience itch in the past month was assessed using ordinal responses from 'never' to 'always'
L1285: Score	Response
0	Never
25	Rarely (1 to a few times per month)
50	Sometimes (1 to a few times per week)
75	Often (1 to a few times per day)
100	Always
L1286: ## *Itch duration*
L1287: The average duration of itching over the last month was assessed using ordinal responses from 'between 0 and 30 min' to 'more than 2 hours'.
L1288: Score	Response
0	Between 0 and 30 min
33.33	Between 30 and 60 min
66.66	Between 1 and 2 hours
100	More than 2 hours
L1289: ## *Itch severity (VAS)*
L1290: Itch severity over the past month was assessed using a VAS with anchors of 'no itch' to 'worst possible itch'.
L1291: Value	Measurement on VAS
Measurement multiplied by 10 (0 to 100)	0 to 10 cm
L1292: ## *Itch consequences (Ordinal Responses)*
L1293: Subjects evaluated the itch consequences over the past month across 11 scenarios using ordinal responses from 'never' to 'always'. The itch consequences score was the average of the scores across the 11 scenarios.
L1294: Scenarios
Lesions from scratching	Needed sleeping pills due to itching
Reduced social contact due to itching	Loss of appetite due to itching
Reduced quality of life due to itching	Bad mood due to itching
Disturbed my routine activities due to itching	Changes in behavior toward others due to itching
L1295: Difficulties in falling asleep due to itching	Loss of concentration due to itching
Waking up due to itching
L1296: Score	Response
0	Never
25	Rarely
50	Sometimes
75	Often
100	Always
L1297: ## *Itch distress (VAS)*
L1298: The itch distress level over the past month was assessed using a VAS with anchors of 'not distressing at all' to 'very distressing'.
L1299: Value	Measurement on VAS
Measurement multiplied by 10 (0 to 100)	0 to 10 cm
L1300: ## *Itch surface area*
L1301: Subjects reported which part of the body itched over the past month across 13 body areas by shading parts of a diagram. A score of 100 was assigned to each selected area and a score of 0 was assigned to each area not selected. The itch surface area score was the average of the scores across the 13 body areas.
L1302: Areas
Face	Arms-hands
Neck	Legs-thighs
Chest	Legs-lower
Abdomen	Legs-feet
Back	Right flank
Arms-upper	Left flank
Arms-lower
L1303: ## Face, Legs, Activity, Cry, Consolability (FLACC) Pain Rating Scale
L1304: Categories	Scoring
0	1	2
Face	No particular expression or smile	Occasional grimace or frown, withdrawn, disinterested	Frequent to constant frown, clenched jaw, quivering chin
Legs	Normal position or relaxed	Uneasy, restless, tense	Kicking, or legs drawn up
Activity	Lying quietly, normal position, moves easily	Squirming, shifting back and forth, tense	Arched, rigid, or jerking
Cry	No cry (awake or asleep)	Moans or whimpers, occasional complaint	Crying steadily, screams or sobs, frequent complaints
Consolability	Content, relaxed	Reassured by occasional touching, hugging, or being talked to, distractable	Difficult to console or comfort
Each of the five categories (F) Face; (L) Legs; (A) Activity; (C) Cry; (C) Consolability is scored from 0-2, which results in a total score between zero and ten.
L1305: Copyright © 2002, The Regents of the University of Michigan. All Rights Reserved.
L1306: Source: BEB-13 Protocol pg. 60

L1310: ## Abbreviated Treatment Satisfaction Questionnaire for Medication
L1311: Instructions: Please take some time to think about how satisfied or dissatisfied you are with the medication you are taking in this clinical trial. We are interested in what you think about the effectiveness, side effects, and convenience experienced when using the medication over the last two to three weeks, or since you last used it. For each question, please place one tick next to the response that most closely corresponds to your own experiences.
L1312: 1. How satisfied or dissatisfied are you with the ability of the medication to prevent or treat your condition?

L1352: Figure 1 displays the Itch Man Scale, a visual representation of itch severity across five levels (0 to 4). Each level is illustrated by a cartoon figure (Itch Man) showing increasing discomfort and red, itchy spots.
Level	Description
0	Comfortable, no itch
1	Itches a little; does not interfere with activity
2	Itches more; sometimes interferes with activity
3	Itches a lot; difficult to be still; concentrate
4	Itches most terribly; impossible to sit still; concentrate
L1353: Product-Limit Failure Curves
With Number of Subjects at Risk
This figure displays the Product-Limit Failure Curves (Kaplan-Meier curves) for Time to First Complete Closure comparing Control Gel and Oleogel-S10. The Y-axis represents Failure Probability (0.0 to 1.0), and the X-axis represents Analysis Value (0 to 100). The curves show the cumulative proportion of subjects who have not yet achieved complete closure.
The legend indicates:
Control Gel (Blue line)
Oleogel-S10 (Red line)
The number of subjects at risk is shown below the X-axis at various analysis values:
Analysis Value	Control Gel (N)	Oleogel-S10 (N)
0	114	109
20	113	107
40	93	85
60	82	66
80	69	57
100	45	38
The Oleogel-S10 curve generally shows a higher failure probability (worse outcome) than the Control Gel curve across the analysis period, although the curves are close together.

L1355: Figure 4 is a histogram showing the density distribution of the number of visits in closure status for Oleogel-S10 and Control groups during the double-blind period. The X-axis represents the Number of Visits (0 to 5), and the Y-axis represents Density (0.0 to 0.7).

Mean values are displayed:
Oleogel-S10: 1.2
Control: 1.1
The distribution shows that both groups have a high density of subjects with 0 visits in closure status. Oleogel-S10 shows a higher density for 2 and 3 visits in closure status compared to the Control group.
L1356: Figure 5 displays two stacked bar charts comparing the subject profiles of target wound closure status by visit for Oleogel-S10 (left) and Control (right) groups. The X-axis represents Study Day (14, 30, 45, 60, 90). The Y-axis represents the proportion of subjects (0 to 1.0). Blue segments represent wound closure status, and yellow segments represent non-closure or missing assessment.
Oleogel-S10: The cumulative proportion of subjects achieving first closure of the target wound by Day 45 is 41%. By Day 90, this proportion increases to 51%.
Control: The cumulative proportion of subjects achieving first closure of the target wound by Day 45 is 29%. By Day 90, this proportion increases to 44%.
L1357: Figure 6 is a line graph titled "Itch Man" showing Mean Itch Man Assessment by Visit in Subjects 4 to 13 Years (Observed Cases). The X-axis represents Study Day (0, 30, 60, 90), and the Y-axis represents Mean (0 to 4). Two groups are compared: Oleogel-S10 (N=55) and Control (N=50).
The Control group starts with a higher mean assessment (around 2.5) and shows a rapid decrease, stabilizing around 1.5 by Day 90. The Oleogel-S10 group starts with a mean assessment around 2.0 and shows a slower, gradual decrease, stabilizing around 1.7 by Day 90. The Oleogel-S10 group consistently maintains a slightly lower mean assessment than the Control group across all measured visits.
Line graph showing Mean Itch Man Assessment by Visit in Subjects 4 to 13 Years (Observed Cases). The graph compares Oleogel-S10 (N=55) and Control (N=50) groups over Study Days 0, 30, 60, and 90. The Control group starts higher (around 2.5) and decreases rapidly, ending around 1.5. The Oleogel-S10 group starts around 2.0 and decreases slowly, ending around 1.7. The Oleogel-S10 group consistently shows a lower mean assessment than the Control group.
L1358: Figure showing six line graphs comparing Oleogel-S10 (N=52) and Control (N=48) across Study Day (0, 30, 60, 90) for six secondary endpoints: Frequency, Duration, Severity, Distress, Surface Area, and Consequences. The Y-axis for all graphs is Mean (0 to 100). The X-axis for all graphs is Study Day (0, 30, 60, 90). The legend indicates Oleogel-S10 (N=52) and Control (N=48).
Frequency: Both groups start around 70 and decrease slightly, stabilizing around 55-60.
Duration: Both groups start around 30 and decrease, with Oleogel-S10 dropping to around 20 at Day 60, then slightly increasing to around 25 at Day 90. Control remains around 25-30.
Severity: Both groups start around 50 and decrease, stabilizing around 40-45.
Distress: Both groups start around 45 and decrease, stabilizing around 35-40.
Surface Area: Both groups start around 35 and decrease, stabilizing around 30-35.
Consequences: Both groups start around 30 and decrease, stabilizing around 25-30.
L1359: Figure 8 displays two line graphs comparing Mean Wong-Baker FACES Pain Scores (Y-axis, 0 to 10) versus Study Day (X-axis, 0, 30, 60, 90) for subjects 4 years of age and older. The graphs are titled "Background Pain" (left) and "Procedural Pain" (right).
Background Pain (Left Graph): The Oleogel-S10 group (N=102, solid line) shows a slight decrease in mean pain score from Day 0 (approx. 3.5) to Day 30 (approx. 2.5), followed by a slight increase to Day 90 (approx. 2.8). The Control group (N=102, dashed line) shows a slight decrease from Day 0 (approx. 3.0) to Day 30 (approx. 2.5), followed by a slight increase to Day 90 (approx. 2.8). Both groups show similar mean pain scores throughout the study period.
Procedural Pain (Right Graph): The Oleogel-S10 group (N=98, solid line) shows a decrease from Day 0 (approx. 3.8) to Day 30 (approx. 2.5), followed by a slight increase to Day 90 (approx. 3.0). The Control group (N=100, dashed line) shows a decrease from Day 0 (approx. 3.0) to Day 30 (approx. 2.0), followed by a slight increase to Day 90 (approx. 2.8). Both groups show similar mean pain scores throughout the study period.

L1361: Line graph showing Treatment Satisfaction Proportion versus Study Day (0, 30, 60, 90) for Oleogel-S10 (N=52) and Control (N=48). The Y-axis is Proportion (0.0 to 1.0). At Day 30, Oleogel-S10 satisfaction is approximately 0.60, and Control is approximately 0.45. At Day 60, Oleogel-S10 satisfaction drops to approximately 0.40, and Control rises to approximately 0.45. At Day 90, Oleogel-S10 satisfaction is approximately 0.38, and Control is approximately 0.50.

L1363: Scatter plot showing Betulin Concentrations (ng/mL) versus BMI (kg/m^2) at Screening (Study BEB-13). The X-axis (BMI) ranges from 10 to 45. The Y-axis (Betulin Level) ranges from 0 to 220. Data points are categorized as Olcegel-310 (open circles) and Control Gel (filled circles). Betulin levels are generally low, mostly below 50 ng/mL, except for a few outliers reaching 100-200 ng/mL, primarily associated with higher BMI values.

The Y-axis (Betulin Level) ranges from 0 to 220. Data points are categorized as Olcegel-310 (open circles) and Control Gel (filled circles). Betulin levels are generally low, mostly below 50 ng/mL, except for a few outliers reaching 100-200 ng/mL, primarily associated with higher Total Wound Area values.
L1365: Wong-Baker FACES® Pain Rating Scale

0
No Hurt
2
Hurts Little Bit
4
Hurts Little More
6
Hurts Even More
8
Hurts Whole Lot
10
Hurts Worst
Six faces representing pain levels 0 through 10. Face 0 is smiling (No Hurt). Face 2 is slightly smiling (Hurts Little Bit). Face 4 is neutral (Hurts Little More). Face 6 is frowning (Hurts Even More). Face 8 is crying (Hurts Whole Lot). Face 10 is crying with tears (Hurts Worst).
